Each of the six boxes $B_1$, $B_2$, $B_3$, $B_4$, $B_5$, $B_6$ initially contains one coin. The following operations are allowed

Type 1) Choose a non-empty box $B_j$, $1\leq j \leq 5$, remove one coin from $B_j$ and add two coins to $B_{j+1}$; 

Type 2) Choose a non-empty box $B_k$, $1\leq k \leq 4$, remove one coin from $B_k$ and swap the contents (maybe empty) of the boxes $B_{k+1}$ and $B_{k+2}$.

Determine if there exists a finite sequence of operations of the allowed types, such that the five boxes $B_1$, $B_2$, $B_3$, $B_4$, $B_5$ become empty, while box $B_6$ contains exactly $2010^{2010^{2010}}$ coins.

[i]

We have 6 boxes B1,...,B6, each initially containing 1 coin.

- Type 2: Choose non-empty B_k (1≤k≤4), remove 1 coin from B_k, swap contents of B_{k+1} and B_{k+2}.

Question: Can we make B1,...,B5 empty and B6 contain exactly 2010^{2010^{2010}} coins?

Let me think about invariants.

Let me denote the state as (c1, c2, c3, c4, c5, c6).

Type 1 on B_j: c_j → c_j - 1, c_{j+1} → c_{j+1} + 2. Net change in total coins: +1.

Type 2 on B_k: c_k → c_k - 1, swap c_{k+1} and c_{k+2}. Net change in total coins: -1.

Let me think about weighted sums. Consider weights w_i and the sum S = Σ w_i c_i.

Type 1 on B_j: ΔS = -w_j + 2 w_{j+1}.
Type 2 on B_k: ΔS = -w_k + (w_{k+2} - w_{k+1}) c_{k+1} + (w_{k+1} - w_{k+2}) c_{k+2} = -w_k + (w_{k+2} - w_{k+1})(c_{k+1} - c_{k+2}).

Then Type 1 on B_j (j=1..5): ΔS = -w_j + 2w_{j+1}.
- j=1: -u + 2v
- j=2..5: -v + 2v = v

- k=1: -u
- k=2..4: -v

Let me think about this. Type 2 on B_k removes 1 from B_k and swaps B_{k+1}, B_{k+2}. This doesn't directly add coins; it removes 1 coin and rearranges. So Type 2 reduces the total coin count by 1.

The idea would be: use Type 2 to rearrange coins in a way that allows more efficient "pumping" of coins to B_6.

Let me think about this differently. Consider the "value" S = 32c1+16c2+8c3+4c4+2c5+c6, invariant under Type 1. To get B_6 = N with B_1=...=B_5=0, we need S = N. Since S starts at 63 and is invariant under Type 1, we need Type 2 operations to increase S from 63 to N.

Type 2 on B_k: ΔS = -(w_k/4)(4 + c_{k+1} - c_{k+2}).

For this to be positive (increase S), we need 4 + c_{k+1} - c_{k+2} < 0, i.e., c_{k+2} > c_{k+1} + 4, i.e., c_{k+2} - c_{k+1} ≥ 5.

Wait, let me reconsider. ΔS = -(w_k/4)(4 + c_{k+1} - c_{k+2}). If c_{k+2} > c_{k+1} + 4, then 4 + c_{k+1} - c_{k+2} < 0, so ΔS > 0. So S increases when the right box has more coins. But after the swap, the right box (B_{k+2}) now has fewer coins (the old c_{k+1}) and the left box (B_{k+1}) has more (the old c_{k+2}). So we've moved coins leftward but increased S. B_4: 1 coin = 4 units of S, gives 1 in exponent. 1 unit of S → 1/4.

So if we split S = 63 into "exponent part" E and "base part" B (with E + B = 63, E divisible by 4... well, E = 4·(4a+c) for some nonneg integers), the result is 2^{E/4} · B.

To maximize 2^{E/4} · B with E + B = 63, E ≡ 0 mod 4 (since E = 4(4a+c) and 4a+c is a nonneg integer), B ≥ 1:

Let f(E) = 2^{E/4} · (63 - E). Take derivative: f'(E) = 2^{E/4} · (ln2/4)(63-E) - 2^{E/4} = 2^{E/4}[(ln2/4)(63-E) - 1].

Set to 0: (ln2/4)(63-E) = 1, so 63 - E = 4/ln2 ≈ 5.77, E ≈ 57.23.

So optimal E ≈ 56 (nearest multiple of 4), B = 7. Result: 2^{14} · 7 = 114688.

Or E = 60, B = 3: 2^{15} · 3 = 98304. Less than 114688.
E = 52, B = 11: 2^{13} · 11 = 90112. Less.

So the maximum with this strategy is 2^{14} · 7 = 114688.

Consider: move a pile from B_6 to B_5 (Type 2 on B_4, costs 1 from B_4), then from B_5 to B_4 (Type 2 on B_3, costs 1 from B_3), then pump B_4 to B_5 to B_6 (Type 1, doubles). But the pile doesn't double by moving from B_4 to B_6; it doubles at each step. From B_4 to B_5: doubles. From B_5 to B_6: doubles. So B_4 → B_6 gives 4x.

But moving B_6 → B_5 → B_4 costs 2 coins (from B_4 and B_3). And the pile stays the same size (swapping doesn't change sizes, just locations). Then pumping B_4 → B_6 gives 4x. So net: pile × 4, cost: 2 coins from B_3 and B_4.

But we also need to "refill" B_3 and B_4 for the next iteration. This requires coins from further left.

Hmm, let me think about this more carefully with a concrete example.

Let me try to set up a self-sustaining loop.

Suppose we have coins in B_1, B_3, B_4, and B_6. The idea:
1. Type 2 on B_4: swap B_5, B_6. Pile moves from B_6 to B_5. (Cost: 1 from B_4)
2. Type 2 on B_3: swap B_4, B_5. Pile moves from B_5 to B_4. (Cost: 1 from B_3)
3. Pump B_4 to B_6 (via B_5): Type 1 on B_4 (pile times), then Type 1 on B_5 (2·pile times). Pile is now 4× original in B_6. (No cost, but B_4 and B_5 are now empty)
4. Refill B_3 and B_4 from B_1: pump B_1 to B_3 (gives 4 coins in B_3 per coin in B_1), then pump some from B_3 to B_4.

But step 4 consumes B_1 coins. So this isn't self-sustaining unless we can also move some of the amplified pile back to B_1.

Moving from B_6 to B_1 would require 5 Type 2 operations (B_4, B_3, B_2, B_1, and... wait, Type 2 on B_k swaps B_{k+1} and B_{k+2}. To move from B_6 to B_5: Type 2 on B_4. B_5 to B_4: Type 2 on B_3. B_4 to B_3: Type 2 on B_2. B_3 to B_2: Type 2 on B_1. B_2 to B_1: no direct operation (Type 2 on B_0 doesn't exist). So we can move a pile from B_6 to B_2 but not to B_1.

Hmm. So we can't move coins all the way back to B_1. The leftmost we can move a pile to is B_2 (using Type 2 on B_1 to swap B_2 and B_3).

But from B_2, we can pump to B_6 with Type 1, getting 2^4 = 16x. And moving from B_6 to B_2 costs 4 Type 2 operations (on B_4, B_3, B_2, B_1), costing 1 coin each from B_4, B_3, B_2, B_1.

So the "cycle" would be: B_6 → B_2 (cost 4 coins) → pump to B_6 (16x). Net: 16x per cycle, cost 4 coins from B_1..B_4.

But we need to refill B_1..B_4. We can't move coins back to B_1 (as noted). So B_1 gets depleted.

Unless... we leave some coins in B_2 when moving the pile, and use those to refill B_1? No, we can't move from B_2 to B_1 either (no operation moves coins leftward into B_1).

Actually, Type 1 moves coins rightward. Type 2 swaps adjacent pairs (shifted). Neither moves coins into B_1 from the right. So B_1 can only decrease.

So any strategy will eventually deplete B_1, and then B_2, etc. The question is how much amplification we can achieve before all boxes except B_6 are empty.

Let me think about this as an optimization problem. We start with S = 63. Each Type 2 operation changes S. We want to maximize B_6 when B_1=...=B_5=0, which means S = B_6.

So we want to maximize S. Each Type 2 operation changes S by ΔS = -(w_k/4)(4 + c_{k+1} - c_{k+2}).

To increase S, we need c_{k+2} > c_{k+1} + 4. The maximum increase per operation is unbounded (if c_{k+2} is huge).

But the issue is that after all operations, we need B_1=...=B_5=0, which means all the "value" is in B_6. The total S at the end equals B_6.

So the question reduces to: what is the maximum achievable S?

Let me look for invariants more carefully.

S = 32c1+16c2+8c3+4c4+2c5+c6 is invariant under Type 1. Initially 63. Target: N = 2010^{2010^{2010}}.

So we need Type 2 operations to change S from 63 to N. The total change from Type 2 is N - 63.

Let me think about S modulo 3.

S mod 3: 32≡2, 16≡1, 8≡2, 4≡1, 2≡2, 1≡1. So S ≡ 2c1+c2+2c3+c4+2c5+c6 mod 3.

Initially: 2+1+2+1+2+1 = 9 ≡ 0 mod 3.
Target: N = 2010^{2010^{2010}}. 2010 ≡ 0 mod 3, so N ≡ 0 mod 3.

So S ≡ 0 mod 3 initially and finally. ΔS ≡ 0 mod 3. Is this a constraint?

Type 1: ΔS = 0. ✓
Type 2: ΔS_k = -(w_k/4)(4 + c_{k+1} - c_{k+2}).

k=1: ΔS = -8(4+c2-c3). Mod 3: -8≡-2≡1, so ΔS ≡ (4+c2-c3) ≡ 1+c2-c3 mod 3.
k=2: ΔS = -4(4+c3-c4). Mod 3: -4≡-1≡2, so ΔS ≡ 2(4+c3-c4) ≡ 2(1+c3-c4) mod 3.
k=3: ΔS = -2(4+c4-c5). Mod 3: -2≡1, so ΔS ≡ (4+c4-c5) ≡ 1+c4-c5 mod 3.
k=4: ΔS = -1(4+c5-c6). Mod 3: -1≡2, so ΔS ≡ 2(4+c5-c6) ≡ 2(1+c5-c6) mod 3.

These are state-dependent, so mod 3 is not an invariant. We can probably achieve any residue mod 3.

Let me think about whether there's a strategy that gives exponential (or double exponential) growth.

Key question: Can we create a self-sustaining amplification loop?

For a loop, we need to move some coins from B_6 back to the left boxes, amplify, and return to B_6 with more coins, while replenishing the left boxes.

The problem is that moving coins leftward (via Type 2) costs coins from the boxes we operate on, and we can't move coins into B_1 from the right.

Wait, actually, can we move coins into B_1? Type 1 on B_1 removes from B_1 and adds to B_2. Type 2 on B_1 removes from B_1 and swaps B_2, B_3. Neither adds to B_1. So B_1 is monotonically non-increasing. Once B_1 = 0, it stays 0.

Similarly, can B_2 increase? Type 1 on B_1 adds 2 to B_2. Type 2 on B_1 swaps B_2 and B_3 (so B_2 can change). So B_2 can increase via Type 1 on B_1, but once B_1 = 0, B_2 can only decrease (via Type 1 on B_2 or Type 2 on B_2) or be swapped with B_3.

So the leftmost box with coins acts as a "resource" that can only be depleted. This suggests that the process must terminate, and the maximum B_6 is finite.

But wait, Type 2 on B_1 swaps B_2 and B_3. If B_3 > B_2, this increases B_2. So B_2 can increase even without B_1, as long as B_3 has coins.

So the pile grows as: $4 \to 16 \to 2^{16} \to 2^{2^{16}} \to \cdots$

This is a tower of exponentials! After $k$ rounds, the pile is a tower of 2's of height roughly $k$.

Total cost from $B_3$: 2 (round 1) + 1 (round 2) + 1 (round 3) + ... = $k + 1$ for $k$ rounds.

So with $n$ coins in $B_3$, we can do about $n - 1$ rounds, giving a tower of 2's of height about $n - 1$.

The final pile (in $B_6$) is approximately a tower of 2's of height $n$.

For $g(3, n) \approx 2^{2^{2^{\cdot^{\cdot^{\cdot}}}}}$ (height $\approx n$).

I think my approximation was wrong. Let me re-derive.

$g(3, n)$: the pile sequence is $a_1 = 4, a_2 = 16, a_3 = 2^{16}, a_4 = 2^{2^{16}}, \ldots$ with $a_{k+1} = 2^{a_k}$ (approximately, ignoring the +1).

So $a_k \approx 2 \uparrow\uparrow (k+1)$ (tower of height $k+1$):
- $a_1 = 4 = 2 \uparrow\uparrow 2$
- $a_2 = 16 = 2 \uparrow\uparrow 3$  (actually $2^4 = 16$, but $2 \uparrow\uparrow 3 = 2^{2^2} = 2^4 = 16$) ✓
- $a_3 = 2^{16} = 2 \uparrow\uparrow 4$ ✓ ($2^{2^{2^2}} = 2^{16}$)
- $a_4 = 2^{2^{16}} = 2 \uparrow\uparrow 5$ ✓

With $n$ coins in $B_3$, we do $n-1$ rounds, and the final pile is $a_{n-1} = 2 \uparrow\uparrow n$ (but in the last round, we apply $g(4, a_{n-2}) = 2^{a_{n-2}+1} \approx 2^{a_{n-2}} = a_{n-1}$).

So $g(3, n) \approx 2 \uparrow\uparrow n$ for $n \geq 2$.

Check: $g(3, 2) = 32 = 2^5$. $2 \uparrow\uparrow 2 = 4$. Not matching. The issue is that $g(3, 2)$ does 1 round: pile = 4, then $g(4, 4) = 2^5 = 32$. And $2 \uparrow\uparrow 2 = 4 \neq 32$.

The discrepancy is because $g(4, m) = 2^{m+1}$, not $2^m$. So the pile after round $k$ is $a_k = 2^{a_{k-1}+1}$, not $2^{a_{k-1}}$. For large $a_{k-1}$, the $+1$ is negligible, so $a_k \approx 2^{a_{k-1}} \approx 2 \uparrow\uparrow (k+1)$.

For small values, the $+1$ matters. But for the asymptotic growth, $g(3, n) \approx 2 \uparrow\uparrow n$.

Now, $g(2, n)$:
- Round 1: pile = 4 in $B_3$. ($\approx 2 \uparrow\uparrow 2$)
- Round 2: $g(3, 4) \approx 2 \uparrow\uparrow 4 = 2^{16}$ in $B_3$.
- Round 3: $g(3, 2^{16}) \approx 2 \uparrow\uparrow 2^{16}$ in $B_3$.
- Round $k$: $g(3, \text{pile}_{k-1}) \approx 2 \uparrow\uparrow \text{pile}_{k-1}$ in $B_3$.

So the pile grows as: $b_1 = 4, b_{k+1} = 2 \uparrow\uparrow b_k$.

$b_1 = 4 = 2 \uparrow\uparrow 2$
$b_2 = 2 \uparrow\uparrow 4 = 2^{16}$
$b_3 = 2 \uparrow\uparrow (2^{16})$
$b_4 = 2 \uparrow\uparrow (2 \uparrow\uparrow 2^{16})$

This is $2 \uparrow\uparrow\uparrow (k+1)$:
- $2 \uparrow\uparrow\uparrow 2 = 2 \uparrow\uparrow 2 = 4 = b_1$ ✓
- $2 \uparrow\uparrow\uparrow 3 = 2 \uparrow\uparrow (2 \uparrow\uparrow\uparrow 2) = 2 \uparrow\uparrow 4 = 2^{16} = b_2$ ✓
- $2 \uparrow\uparrow\uparrow 4 = 2 \uparrow\uparrow (2 \uparrow\uparrow\uparrow 3) = 2 \uparrow\uparrow 2^{16} = b_3$ ✓

So $g(2, n) \approx 2 \uparrow\uparrow\uparrow (n+1)$.

Let me think about what $S$ values are achievable.

Consider a simplified version: just boxes $B_4, B_5, B_6$ (with $B_1 = B_2 = B_3 = 0$). We start with some coins in $B_4$ and $B_6$ (and 0 in $B_5$). $S = 4c_4 + c_6$ (since $c_5 = 0$).

Type 1 on $B_4$: $c_4 \to c_4 - 1$, $c_5 \to c_5 + 2$. $\Delta S = 0$.
Type 1 on $B_5$: $c_5 \to c_5 - 1$, $c_6 \to c_6 + 2$. $\Delta S = 0$.
Type 2 on $B_4$: $c_4 \to c_4 - 1$, swap $c_5, c_6$. $\Delta S = -(4 + c_5 - c_6)$.

If $c_5 = 0$ and $c_6 = n$: $\Delta S = -(4 - n) = n - 4$. So $S$ increases by $n - 4$.

After the swap: $c_5 = n, c_6 = 0$. Then pump $B_5$ to $B_6$: $c_6 = 2n$. $S = 2n$ (since $c_4$ decreased by 1, $c_5 = 0$, $c_6 = 2n$). But $S$ was $4(c_4 - 1) + 2n = 4c_4 - 4 + 2n$. And before the Type 2, $S = 4c_4 + n$. So $\Delta S = 4c_4 - 4 + 2n - 4c_4 - n = n - 4$. ✓

So each Type 2 on $B_4$ (when $c_5 = 0, c_6 = n$) increases $S$ by $n - 4$ and doubles $c_6$.

If we start with $(d, 0, n)$ (i.e., $c_4 = d, c_5 = 0, c_6 = n$) and do $d$ Type 2 operations on $B_4$ (each followed by pumping $B_5$ to $B_6$):

After 1st: $c_6 = 2n$, $c_4 = d-1$, $S$ increased by $n - 4$.
After 2nd: $c_6 = 4n$, $c_4 = d-2$, $S$ increased by $2n - 4$.
After 3rd: $c_6 = 8n$, $c_4 = d-3$, $S$ increased by $4n - 4$.
...
After $j$-th: $c_6 = 2^j n$, $c_4 = d-j$, $S$ increased by $2^{j-1} n - 4$.

Total $\Delta S$ after $d$ operations: $\sum_{j=1}^{d} (2^{j-1} n - 4) = n(2^d - 1) - 4d$.

Final $S = (4d + n) + n(2^d - 1) - 4d = n \cdot 2^d$.

And final $c_6 = 2^d \cdot n$, $c_4 = 0$. So $S = c_6 = 2^d \cdot n$. ✓

So from $(d, 0, n)$ with $S = 4d + n$, we get $B_6 = 2^d \cdot n$ with $S = 2^d \cdot n$.

The achievable $B_6$ values (from this simple strategy) are $2^d \cdot n$ where $4d + n = 63$, i.e., $n = 63 - 4d$, so $B_6 = 2^d (63 - 4d)$.

These are: $d=0: 63$, $d=1: 118$, $d=2: 220$, ..., $d=14: 114688$, $d=15: 98304$.

- Coin in $B_1$: contributes 32 to $B_6$

Let me think about whether we can "fine-tune" the amplification to hit a specific target.

In the $g(3, n)$ strategy, each round roughly squares the pile (or doubles the tower height). By choosing the number of Type 2 operations within each round, we can achieve different values.

More specifically, in each round of the $g(3, n)$ strategy:
1. We have $m$ coins in $B_4$ and a pile of $p$ in $B_6$.
2. We do $j$ Type 2 operations on $B_4$ ($0 \leq j \leq m$), getting $B_6 = 2^j \cdot p + 4(m - j)$.
3. We move the pile to $B_4$ (via Type 2 on $B_3$) for the next round.

By choosing $j$ (the number of Type 2 operations in each round), we can achieve different values of the pile. This gives us a lot of freedom.

But is this freedom enough to hit $M$ exactly?

Let me think about a simpler version. Suppose we have $m$ coins in $B_4$ and $p$ in $B_6$. We can achieve $B_6 = 2^j \cdot p + 4(m-j)$ for any $0 \leq j \leq m$. The set of achievable values is $\{2^j \cdot p + 4(m-j) : 0 \leq j \leq m\}$.

For large $p$, the dominant term is $2^j \cdot p$, and the $4(m-j)$ term is a small correction. So the achievable values are approximately $\{2^j \cdot p : 0 \leq j \leq m\}$, which are $p, 2p, 4p, \ldots, 2^m p$.

If we want to hit a target $T$, we need $T \approx 2^j \cdot p$ for some $j$, i.e., $j \approx \log_2(T/p)$. If $\log_2(T/p)$ is an integer and $0 \leq j \leq m$, we can hit $T$ approximately. The correction term $4(m-j)$ allows us to fine-tune.

But the correction is only $4(m-j)$, which is at most $4m$. So we can only fine-tune within a range of $4m$ around $2^j \cdot p$.

For the next round, the pile is $2^j \cdot p + 4(m-j)$, and we have new coins in $B_4$ (from the next round's Type 1 on $B_3$). The fine-tuning range grows with each round.

This suggests that we can achieve any value within a certain range, and the range grows with each round. If the range eventually covers $M$, we can hit $M$.

The total number of achievable values is roughly $d^2 / 2$ (the number of valid $(j, j')$ pairs), and they span a range of roughly $8d$. So the density is roughly $d/16$, which means for large $d$, the achievable values cover a significant fraction of the range.

Let me reconsider. From $(0, 0, 0, d-1, n-k, 2k)$ (after Type 2 and partial pump), instead of pumping all of $B_5$ to $B_6$, we can do another Type 2 on $B_4$:

This gives $B_6 \in \{n, n+3, n+6, \ldots, 4n\}$, with spacing 3.

Now, from $(0, 0, 0, d-2, 0, n+3k)$, we can do another pair:
$B_6 = (n+3k) + 3k'$ for $0 \leq k' \leq n+3k$, with $S = S + 3k - 8 + 3k' - 8$.

So after two pairs (4 Type 2 operations): $B_6 = n + 3k + 3k'$ for $0 \leq k \leq n$ and $0 \leq k' \leq n + 3k$.

The achievable $B_6$ values are: $n + 3(k + k')$ for valid $(k, k')$. Since $k + k'$ can range from 0 to $n + (n + 3n) = 5n$ (when $k = n, k' = 4n$), the achievable values are $n, n+3, n+6, \ldots, n + 15n = 16n$, with spacing 3.

But actually, not all multiples of 3 in this range are achievable, because $k'$ is constrained by $k' \leq n + 3k$. For a target $B_6 = n + 3m$, we need $k + k' = m$ with $0 \leq k \leq n$ and $0 \leq k' \leq n + 3k$, i.e., $k' = m - k$ and $0 \leq m - k \leq n + 3k$, i.e., $k \leq m$ and $k \geq (m - n)/4$.

For $m \leq n$: $k$ can be 0 to $m$, and $k' = m - k \leq m \leq n \leq n + 3k$. So all $m$ from 0 to $n$ are achievable.

For $n < m \leq 4n$: $k \geq (m-n)/4$ and $k \leq n$. Since $(m-n)/4 \leq (4n-n)/4 = 3n/4 < n$, there exists a valid $k$. So all $m$ from 0 to $4n$ are achievable.

For $4n < m \leq 5n$: $k \geq (m-n)/4 > (4n-n)/4 = 3n/4$ and $k \leq n$ and $k' = m - k \leq n + 3k$, i.e., $m - k \leq n + 3k$, i.e., $m - n \leq 4k$, i.e., $k \geq (m-n)/4$. And $k \leq n$. And $k' = m - k \geq 0$, i.e., $k \leq m$. Since $m \leq 5n$ and $k \leq n$, $k \leq n \leq m$. So we need $(m-n)/4 \leq k \leq n$, which has a solution iff $(m-n)/4 \leq n$, i.e., $m \leq 5n$. ✓

But the key point is: $S$ grows, and the pile grows even faster. After a few rounds, both are enormous.

Now, the key question: can we control $S$ to hit a specific target?

The issue is that at each round, $S$ increases by a specific amount (determined by the pile size). We can't freely choose $\Delta S$; it's determined by the pile.

But we CAN choose how many Type 2 operations to do within each round. Instead of doing all $m-1$ Type 2 on $B_4$ (where $m$ is the pile in $B_4$), we can do fewer, leaving some coins in $B_4$ and getting a smaller pile.

This gives us control over $\Delta S$ at each round. By choosing the number of Type 2 operations, we can achieve different $\Delta S$ values.

But the granularity of this control depends on the pile size. For large piles, the $\Delta S$ values are widely spaced (growing exponentially), so we can't hit every integer.

However, we can use the fine-tuning strategy (partial pumping) within each Type 2 on $B_4$ to get finer control. As I showed earlier, by choosing how many coins to pump from $B_5$ to $B_6$ between Type 2 operations, we can achieve $\Delta S$ with spacing 3 (or even 1 with more complex strategies).

OK, I think I've spent enough time on this analysis. Let me now think about the overall structure of the proof and write it up.

The answer is YES, we can achieve $B_6 = 2010^{2010^{2010}}$.

The proof has two main parts:
1. We can amplify $S$ to a very large value (much larger than $N$).
2. From a large $S$, we can fine-tune to hit $N$ exactly.

For part 1, the amplification strategy (using Type 2 on $B_3, B_2, B_1$) gives towers of exponentials, much larger than $N$.

For part 2, the fine-tuning strategy (using Type 2 on $B_4$ with partial pumping) allows us to achieve any value $\equiv 0 \pmod{3}$ in a range around $S$. Since $N \equiv 0 \pmod{3}$, we can hit $N$.

But I need to make this more rigorous. Let me think about the key lemma.

Key Lemma: From any state $(0, 0, 0, d, 0, n)$ with $d \geq 2$ and $n \geq 5$, we can achieve $B_6 = n + 3k$ for any $0 \leq k \leq n$, using 2 Type 2 operations on $B_4$ (and some Type 1 operations).

Proof: 
1. Type 2 on $B_4$: $(0, 0, 0, d-1, n, 0)$. (Swap $B_5 = 0$ and $B_6 = n$.)
2. Pump $k$ coins from $B_5$ to $B_6$: $(0, 0, 0, d-1, n-k, 2k)$.
3. Type 2 on $B_4$: $(0, 0, 0, d-2, 2k, n-k)$. (Swap $B_5 = n-k$ and $B_6 = 2k$.)
4. Pump all from $B_5$ to $B_6$: $(0, 0, 0, d-2, 0, n-k+4k) = (0, 0, 0, d-2, 0, n+3k)$.

$S = 4(d-2) + n + 3k$. ✓

So from $B_6 = n$ with $d$ coins in $B_4$, we can achieve $B_6 = n + 3k$ for $0 \leq k \leq n$, using 2 coins from $B_4$.

Now, from $B_6 = n + 3k$ with $d - 2$ coins in $B_4$, we can repeat: achieve $B_6 = (n + 3k) + 3k'$ for $0 \leq k' \leq n + 3k$, using 2 more coins from $B_4$.

After $r$ applications: $B_6 = n + 3m$ for any $0 \leq m \leq f(r) \cdot n$ where $f(r) = (4^r - 1)/3$ (as computed earlier). Using $2r$ coins from $B_4$.

So after $r$ applications, we can achieve any $B_6 \equiv n \pmod{3}$ in $[n, 4^r \cdot n]$, using $2r$ coins from $B_4$.

Now, the key: if $n \equiv 0 \pmod{3}$ and $N \equiv 0 \pmod{3}$, then $N = n + 3m$ for $m = (N-n)/3$. We need $m \leq (4^r - 1)/3 \cdot n$, i.e., $N \leq 4^r \cdot n$.

And we need $2r \leq d$, i.e., $r \leq d/2$.

So we need $4^{d/2} \cdot n \geq N$, i.e., $2^d \cdot n \geq N$.

With $4d + n = S$: $n = S - 4d$. So $2^d (S - 4d) \geq N$.

We need to choose $S$ and $d$ such that:
1. $S$ is achievable (via amplification).
2. $2^d (S - 4d) \geq N$.
3. $S - 4d \equiv 0 \pmod{3}$.
4. $S - 4d \geq 3$ (so that $n \geq 3$).

For condition 3: $S \equiv 4d \equiv d \pmod{3}$. We can choose $d \equiv S \pmod{3}$.

For condition 2: the optimal $d$ is roughly $\log_2(N/n) \approx \log_2 N$ (since $n$ is small compared to $N$). And $S \approx 4d \approx 4 \log_2 N$.

$\log_2 N = \log_2(2010^{2010^{2010}}) = 2010^{2010} \cdot \log_2 2010 \approx 2010^{2010} \cdot 11$.

So $S \approx 44 \cdot 2010^{2010}$.

Now, can we achieve $S \approx 44 \cdot 2010^{2010}$ via amplification?

From the amplification analysis, starting from $(1, 1, 1, 1, 1, 1)$ with $S = 63$:

Using the $g(3, n)$ strategy (Type 2 on $B_3$), we can achieve $S$ values that grow as towers of exponentials. After just 3-4 rounds, $S$ is much larger than $44 \cdot 2010^{2010}$.

But we need to achieve $S \approx 44 \cdot 2010^{2010}$ EXACTLY (or at least, an $S$ value that satisfies the conditions).

The amplification strategy produces specific $S$ values, not arbitrary ones. But we can use the fine-tuning (choosing the number of Type 2 operations within each round) to achieve nearby values.

Hmm, but the fine-tuning gives us values with spacing 3 (or larger), not spacing 1. So we might not be able to hit $S = 44 \cdot 2010^{2010}$ exactly.

But wait, we don't need to hit $S$ exactly. We need $S$ to satisfy:
1. $S \geq 4d + 3$ for some $d$ with $2^d \cdot 3 \geq N$.
2. $S \equiv d \pmod{3}$ for some valid $d$.

Condition 1: $S \geq 4 \lceil \log_2(N/3) \rceil + 3 \approx 44 \cdot 2010^{2010}$.

Condition 2: for any $S$, we can choose $d \equiv S \pmod{3}$ with $d \leq (S-3)/4$.

So we just need $S \geq 44 \cdot 2010^{2010}$ (approximately), and $S$ can be any value (we'll choose $d$ appropriately).

Now, the amplification strategy can produce $S$ values much larger than $44 \cdot 2010^{2010}$. But can it produce a value $\geq 44 \cdot 2010^{2010}$?
